=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array nums of n integers, and a 2D integer array queries of size q, where queries[i] = [l_i, r_i].
For each query, you must find the maximum XOR score of any subarray of nums[l_i..r_i].
The XOR score of an array a is found by repeatedly applying the following operations on a so that only one element remains, that is the score:

Simultaneously replace a[i] with a[i] XOR a[i + 1] for all indices i except the last one.
Remove the last element of a.

Return an array answer of size q where answer[i] is the answer to query i.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,8,4,32,16,1], queries = [[0,2],[1,4],[0,5]]
Output: [12,60,60]
Explanation:
In the first query, nums[0..2] has 6 subarrays [2], [8], [4], [2, 8], [8, 4], and [2, 8, 4] each with a respective XOR score of 2, 8, 4, 10, 12, and 6. The answer for the query is 12, the largest of all XOR scores.
In the second query, the subarray of nums[1..4] with the largest XOR score is nums[1..4] with a score of 60.
In the third query, the subarray of nums[0..5] with the largest XOR score is nums[1..4] with a score of 60.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [0,7,3,2,8,5,1], queries = [[0,3],[1,5],[2,4],[2,6],[5,6]]
Output: [7,14,11,14,5]
Explanation:



Index
nums[l_i..r_i]
Maximum XOR Score Subarray
Maximum Subarray XOR Score




0
[0, 7, 3, 2]
[7]
7


1
[7, 3, 2, 8, 5]
[7, 3, 2, 8]
14


2
[3, 2, 8]
[3, 2, 8]
11


3
[3, 2, 8, 5, 1]
[2, 8, 5, 1]
14


4
[5, 1]
[5]
5




 
Constraints:

1 <= n == nums.length <= 2000
0 <= nums[i] <= 2^31 - 1
1 <= q == queries.length <= 10^5
queries[i].length == 2 
queries[i] = [l_i, r_i]
0 <= l_i <= r_i <= n - 1

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's understand problem. We have array nums length n <= 2000, q <= 1e5. Need for each subarray range [l,r], maximum XOR score of any subarray of nums[l..r]. XOR score of array a: repeatedly replace adjacent XORs and remove last until one element remains. Need formula.

Given array a length m. Operation: new b length m-1 with b[i] = a[i] XOR a[i+1], remove last. Repeat. What is final element? This is like binomial transform over XOR. For original a[0..m-1], final score = XOR over i of C(m-1, i) mod 2 * a[i]? Let's verify. Each element contributes to final with coefficient equal to number of paths? Operation computes finite differences over GF(2). After k steps, b_i = XOR_{j=0..k} C(k,j) a[i+j] (Pascal mod2). Final after m-1 steps at index 0: XOR_{j=0..m-1} C(m-1,j) a[j] mod2. Since XOR addition, coefficient parity. C(m-1,j) odd iff j bitwise subset of (m-1): (j & ~(m-1))==0. So score of subarray nums[i..j] length L = j-i+1, m=L: XOR of nums[i+t] for t subset of L-1? Actually C(L-1,t) odd iff (t & (L-1)) == t (since C parity Lucas: t subset of L-1). So score = XOR_{t subset of L-1} nums[i+t]. Let's test example [2,8,4], L=3, L-1=2 binary 10, t subset {0,2}; score = nums[0] XOR nums[2] = 2 XOR4=6. Yes. [2,8] L=2, L-1=1, t {0,1}: 2 XOR8=10. Good. [8,4] L=2: 8 XOR4=12. [2,8,4] score 6. [7,3,2,8] L=4, L-1=3 binary 11, t 0..3 all, XOR=7^3^2^8=14. Good.

Need for each query [l,r], find maximum over all subarrays [x,y] within [l,r] of score S(x,y) = XOR_{t subset of y-x} nums[x+t], where y-x = len-1 = d, 0<=d<=r-l. Need max value.

n <= 2000. Need q 1e5. Need precompute something O(n^2 log?) maybe.

That is maximum over all subarrays contained in interval. We can precompute best[l][r] = max score of any subarray inside nums[l..r]. Need answer queries O(1). n=2000 => n^2=4e6, okay. Let's explore if in-place dp update actually computes score for all d, not just d increments by 1. We tested. Need formal: Let A_d[i] = XOR_{t subset d} nums[i+t]. Then A_d[i] = A_{d-1}[i] XOR A_{d-1}[i+1]. Need verify identity for all d. Let's prove using binomial coefficients mod2:
A_d[i] = ⊕_{j=0}^d C(d,j) mod2 * nums[i+j].
A_{d-1}[i] ⊕ A_{d-1}[i+1] = ⊕_{j=0}^{d-1} C(d-1,j) nums[i+j] ⊕ ⊕_{j=1}^{d} C(d-1,j-1) nums[i+j].
Coefficient for nums[i+j] is C(d-1,j) ⊕ C(d-1,j-1) mod2. Pascal mod2: C(d,j) ≡ C(d-1,j) + C(d-1,j-1) mod2. Yes. Boundary C(d-1,d)=0, C(d-1,-1)=0. So identity. Great.

Let's think about code style:
```
from typing import List
from array import array

class Solution:
    def maximumSubarrayXor(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)
        best = [None] * (n + 1)
        dp = nums[:]
        best[1] = array('I', dp)
        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            d = length - 1
            # transform dp into scores for this length
            for i in range(n - d):
                dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]
            prev = best[length - 1]
            cnt = n - length + 1
            cur = array('I', [0]) * cnt
            # local variables for speed?
            for i in range(cnt):
                v = dp[i]
                if prev[i] > v:
                    v = prev[i]
                if prev[i + 1] > v:
                    v = prev[i + 1]
                cur[i] = v
            best[length] = cur
        return [best[r - l + 1][l] for l, r in queries]
```

Now, let's think about if there is a possible bug in best recurrence for intervals length L: We used `prev[i]` and `prev[i+1]`. But `prev` is best for length L-1. For interval [i, i+L-1], subarrays not whole:

Let's maybe think about if there is an even simpler DP:

Now, let's think about if there is a possible bug in in-place update due to Python's `^=` operator with list elements and `dp[i+1]` maybe overwritten if loop direction reversed. We use increasing. Let's prove with example d=2:
Before update for d=2, dp = [A1[0], A1[1], A1[2], A1[3], stale?]. Loop i=0: dp0 = A1[0]^A1[1] = A2[0]; dp1 still A1[1]. i=1: dp1 = A1[1]^A1[2] = A2[1]; dp2 still A1[2]. i=2: dp2 = A1[2]^A1[3] = A2[2]. Good. It never uses dp0. So increasing safe. If loop decreasing, would break. Good.

Potential issue: `dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` for i in range(n - d) where n-d is count. For d=1, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. For d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. But after d=1, dp length n, with dp[n-1] stale. For d=2, loop i=0..n-3, uses dp[i+1] up to dp[n-2], which is current. Good. It doesn't use dp[n-1]. So stale doesn't matter. For d=n-1, n-d=1, loop i=0, uses dp[1] current from previous d=n-2? Let's check after d=n-2, current length n-(n-2)=2? Wait d=n-2 corresponds length n-1, current length n-d =2? Actually after update for d=n-2, dp[0:2] current A_{n-2}[0], A_{n-2}[1]; dp[2] stale. For d=n-1, loop range(1): dp0 ^= dp1 -> A_{n-1}[0]. Good. best[n] count=1. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Let's think about if we can combine update and best in one loop? Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Simultaneously replace a[i] with a[i] XOR a[i + 1] for all indices i except the last one. Remove the last element." If you remove last element after replacement, the last element of original is removed, but what about the replacement of a[m-2] uses original a[m-2] and original a[m-1]; yes. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Let's think about if we can combine update and best in one loop? Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Simultaneously replace a[i] with a[i] XOR a[i + 1] for all indices i except the last one. Remove the last element." If you remove last element after replacement, the last element of original is removed, but what about the replacement of a[m-2] uses original a[m-2] and original a[m-1]; yes. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Let's think about if we can combine update and best in one loop? Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Simultaneously replace a[i] with a[i] XOR a[i + 1] for all indices i except the last one. Remove the last element." If you remove last element after replacement, the last element of original is removed, but what about the replacement of a[m-2] uses original a[m-2] and original a[m-1]; yes. Good.

Potential issue: The in-place update `for i in range(n - d): dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]` when `d = length - 1`; for length=2, n-d = n-1, updates indices 0..n-2. But after update, dp[n-1] remains old. Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. For length=3, d=2, n-d = n-2, updates 0..n-3. dp[n-2] is current from length2, dp[n-1] stale. best loop accesses dp[0..n-3], okay. Good.

Then `cnt = n - length + 1 = n-1`; best loop accesses dp[0..n-2], okay. Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Simultaneously replace a[i] with a[i] XOR a[i + 1] for all indices i except the last one. Remove the last element." If you remove last element after replacement, the last element of original is removed, but what about the replacement of a[m-2] uses original a[m-2] and original a[m-1]; yes. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from array import array

class Solution:
    def maximumSubarrayXor(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(nums)

        # best[length][start] = maximum XOR score of any subarray
        # inside nums[start : start + length]
        best = [None] * (n + 1)

        # dp will store the XOR score of the whole subarray of the current length.
        # Initially length = 1.
        dp = nums[:]
        best[1] = array('I', dp)

        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            d = length - 1

            # Transform dp from scores for length-1 to scores for length:
            # score[i][d] = score[i][d-1] XOR score[i+1][d-1]
            for i in range(n - d):
                dp[i] ^= dp[i + 1]

            prev = best[length - 1]
            cnt = n - length + 1
            cur = array('I', [0]) * cnt

            for i in range(cnt):
                v = dp[i]
                if prev[i] > v:
                    v = prev[i]
                if prev[i + 1] > v:
                    v = prev[i + 1]
                cur[i] = v

            best[length] = cur

        return [best[r - l + 1][l] for l, r in queries]
```
